        - generic [ref=e168]:
          - region "Abstract" [ref=e169]:
            - generic [ref=e170]:
              - heading "Abstract" [level=2] [ref=e171]
              - paragraph [ref=e173]:
                - text: The NADPH/NADP
                - superscript [ref=e174]: +
                - text: redox couple is central to metabolism and redox signalling. NADP redox state is differentially regulated by distinct enzymatic machineries at the subcellular compartment level. Nonetheless, a detailed understanding of subcellular NADP redox dynamics is limited by the availability of appropriate tools. Here, we introduce NAPstars, a family of genetically encoded, fluorescent protein-based NADP redox state biosensors. NAPstars offer real-time, specific measurements, across a broad-range of NADP redox states, with subcellular resolution. NAPstar measurements in yeast, plants, and mammalian cell models, reveal a conserved robustness of cytosolic NADP redox homoeostasis. NAPstars uncover cell cycle-linked NADP redox oscillations in yeast and illumination- and hypoxia-dependent NADP redox changes in plant leaves. By applying NAPstars in combination with selective impairment of the glutathione and thioredoxin antioxidative pathways under acute oxidative challenge, we find an unexpected and conserved role for the glutathione system as the primary mediator of antioxidative electron flux.

          - generic [ref=e201]:
            - generic [ref=e203]:
              - heading "Introduction" [level=2] [ref=e204]
              - generic [ref=e205]:
                - paragraph [ref=e206]:
                  - text: Nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide phosphate (NADP), in its reduced (NADPH) and oxidised (NADP
                  - superscript [ref=e207]: +
                  - text: ) states, constitutes a central metabolic redox couple, which is found in all living organisms. NADPH plays a crucial role as an electron donor in numerous, typically anabolic, pathways, for example fatty acid and cholesterol synthesis and photosynthetic carbon assimilation, and for the enzymatic reduction of certain reactive oxygen species, including H
                  - subscript [ref=e208]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e209]: "2"
                  - text: . NADPH also serves as the source of electrons for NADPH oxidases. This family of enzymes is central to the respiratory burst in immune responses and the generation of H
                  - subscript [ref=e210]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e211]: "2"
                  - text: as a second messenger in cellular signalling, development, and environmental sensing across kingdoms
                  - superscript [ref=e212]:
                    - link "Reference 1" [ref=e213] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR1
                      - text: "1"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "Reference 2" [ref=e214] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR2
                      - text: "2"
                  - text: .
                - paragraph [ref=e215]:
                  - text: Since its discovery in the early 1930s
                  - superscript [ref=e216]:
                    - link "Reference 3" [ref=e217] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR3
                      - text: "3"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "Reference 4" [ref=e218] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR4
                      - text: "4"
                  - text: ", NADP, together with its cellular counterpart, NAD, has been extensively investigated. However, our understanding of subcellular NAD(P) redox dynamics is still remarkably incomplete, mainly due to a lack of techniques allowing specific monitoring in defined subcellular compartments in vivo."
                - paragraph [ref=e219]:
                  - text: The use of novel genetically encoded sensors allowed accurate measurements of the subcellular dynamics of numerous metabolites, metals ions including free Ca
                  - superscript [ref=e220]: 2+
                  - text: ", H"
                  - subscript [ref=e221]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e222]: "2"
                  - text: ", and pH"
                  - superscript [ref=e223]:
                    - link "5" [ref=e224] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#ref-CR5"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "6" [ref=e225] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#ref-CR6"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "7" [ref=e226] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#ref-CR7"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "Reference 8" [ref=e227] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR8
                      - text: "8"
                  - text: . Such sensors have revolutionised our understanding of countless previously intractable biological processes, particularly when dealing with small subcellular compartment-specific dynamics, and have underpinned numerous novel discoveries. Genetically encoded sensors for pyridine nucleotides were relative latecomers; however, significant progress has been made over the past decade in developing sensors for NADH
                  - superscript [ref=e228]:
                    - link "Reference 9" [ref=e229] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR9
                      - text: "9"
                  - text: ", NAD"
                  - superscript [ref=e230]: +
                  - superscript [ref=e231]:
                    - link "Reference 10" [ref=e232] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR10
                      - text: "10"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "Reference 11" [ref=e233] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR11
                      - text: "11"
                  - text: ", NADH/NAD"
                  - superscript [ref=e234]: +
                  - superscript [ref=e235]:
                    - link "12" [ref=e236] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#ref-CR12"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "13" [ref=e237] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#ref-CR13"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "Reference 14" [ref=e238] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR14
                      - text: "14"
                  - text: ", NADPH"
                  - superscript [ref=e239]:
                    - link "Reference 15" [ref=e240] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR15
                      - text: "15"
                  - text: ", and NADP"
                  - superscript [ref=e241]: +
                  - superscript [ref=e242]:
                    - link "Reference 16" [ref=e243] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR16
                      - text: "16"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "Reference 17" [ref=e244] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR17
                      - text: "17"
                  - text: . Nonetheless, existing NADPH sensors face major limitations, including sensitivity to pH, a lack of responsiveness to NADP
                  - superscript [ref=e245]: +
                  - text: ", a low signal-to-noise ratio, and a lack of compatibility with alternative measurement techniques like FLIM. A recently created sensor, NERNST, was developed, which purported to be the first NADP redox state sensor, i.e. a sensor of the NADPH/NADP"
                  - superscript [ref=e246]: +
                  - text: ratio
                  - superscript [ref=e247]:
                    - link "Reference 18" [ref=e248] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR18
                      - text: "18"
                  - text: . Nonetheless, considerable concerns arise regarding the specificity of NERNST due to its dependence on a redox-sensitive green fluorescent protein (roGFP2) reporter, which is known to efficiently equilibrate with the glutathione redox couple in vivo
                  - superscript [ref=e249]:
                    - link "19" [ref=e250] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#ref-CR19"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "20" [ref=e251] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#ref-CR20"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "21" [ref=e252] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#ref-CR21"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "Reference 22" [ref=e253] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR22
                      - text: "22"
                  - text: .
                - paragraph [ref=e254]:
                  - text: In this study, we employed a rational probe design strategy to create the NAPstar family of NADP redox state probes. The NAPstar family allows the monitoring of NADP redox states across a 5000-fold range, spanning NADPH/NADP
                  - superscript [ref=e255]: +
                  - text: ratios from approximately 0.001 to 5. We showed that NAPstars facilitate specific and real-time monitoring of subcellular NADP redox state dynamics, which can be measured either by monitoring changes in fluorescence excitation and emission or through fluorescence lifetime imaging. By applying NAPstars in yeast,human cells and plants, we found a surprisingly oxidised cytosolic NADP redox state, albeit with a remarkable robustness to oxidative challenges in yeast. We used NAPstars to reveal oscillations in the NADP redox state associated with cell division and metabolic cycles in yeast and to monitor NADP redox dynamics influenced by illumination and hypoxia–reoxygenation in plants. Finally, we revealed a predominant role of the glutathione system in mediating antioxidative electron flux in response to oxidative challenges that is conserved across diverse eukaryotic cells.
            - generic [ref=e257]:
              - heading "Results" [level=2] [ref=e258]
              - generic [ref=e259]:
                - heading "Peredox mutagenesis delivers a family of NADP redox state sensors" [level=3] [ref=e260]
                - paragraph [ref=e261]:
                  - text: We used the NAD redox state sensor, Peredox-mCherry (henceforth referred to as Peredox)
                  - superscript [ref=e262]:
                    - link "Reference 13" [ref=e263] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR13
                      - text: "13"
                  - text: ", as a chassis for the development of NADP redox state probes (Fig."
                  - link "1a" [ref=e264] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig1
                  - text: ). Peredox incorporates a circularly permuted T-Sapphire (TS) fluorescent protein nested between two copies of the NADH/NAD
                  - superscript [ref=e265]: +
                  - text: "-binding domain of the bacterial transcriptional repressor Rex"
                  - superscript [ref=e266]:
                    - link "Reference 13" [ref=e267] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR13
                      - text: "13"
                  - text: . Structural changes, contingent on whether the Rex domains bind to NAD
                  - superscript [ref=e268]: +
                  - text: or NADH, induce changes in the TS fluorescence. This fluorescence change can be normalised against the signal from a C-terminally fused mCherry (mC) fluorescent protein. Peredox offers several advantages over other NADH and NADPH sensors, including limited pH sensitivity and the high apparent brightness of TS-based probes in biological systems relative to some cpYFP-based sensors such as SoNar and the iNap family
                  - superscript [ref=e269]:
                    - link "Reference 12" [ref=e270] [cursor=pointer]:

                - paragraph [ref=e300]:
                  - text: To alter the binding pocket of Peredox to favour NADP binding, we introduced mutations known to switch the specificity of the Rex domain from favouring NADH binding to favouring NADPH
                  - superscript [ref=e301]:
                    - link "Reference 15" [ref=e302] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR15
                      - text: "15"
                  - text: and generated combinatorial mutants thereof. These mutations were applied equally to each of the Rex domains of Peredox, resulting in a family of constructs termed NAPstars (Supplementary Table
                  - link "1" [ref=e303] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#MOESM1
                  - text: ). To gain insight into the potential structure of NAPstar, we used AlphaFold2 with the 919 amino acids of NAPstar3 as the input (Supplementary Information). The sequence consists of a Rex domain linked with an SAAGGH amino acid sequence to a circular permutated TS, followed by a single amino acid Thr linked to a second Rex domain, a GSGTGGNASDGGGSGG linker, and the mCherry sequence. Five models were generated, and the top-ranked model displayed a reliable structure, with an average predicted Local Distance Difference Test (pLDDT) score of 87.8% (Fig.
                  - link "1b" [ref=e304] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig1
                  - text: ).
                - paragraph [ref=e305]:
                  - text: For in vitro characterisation, NAPstar1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 7 and NAPstarC were expressed as recombinant proteins in E. coli. Fluorescence spectra were recorded in the presence of 0–100 µM NADPH (Supplementary Fig.
                  - link "1" [ref=e306] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#MOESM1
                  - text: ). NAPstars1, 2, 3, 6 and 7 exhibited pronounced NADPH-dependent changes in TS fluorescence excitation and emission spectra, with excitation and emission maxima at approximately 400 and 515 nm, respectively, and with a spectroscopic dynamic range of approximately 2.5, i.e. about 0.4 units on a log10 scale, similar to Peredox, (Fig.
                  - link "1c" [ref=e307] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig1
                  - text: )
                  - superscript [ref=e308]:
                    - link "Reference 23" [ref=e309] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR23
                      - text: "23"
                  - text: . NAPstarC, which contains mutations preventing nucleotide binding to Rex, remained unresponsive to changes in the NADPH concentration (Supplementary Fig.
                  - link "1a, b" [ref=e310] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#MOESM1
                  - text: ). Similarly, NAPstar4 did not show any NADPH-dependent change in the tested range and was therefore excluded from further characterisation. Changes in NADPH concentration did not affect the fluorescence excitation or emission of mC (Supplementary Fig.
                  - link "1d, f, h, j, l, n" [ref=e311] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#MOESM1
                  - text: ). Our results thus demonstrate that NAPstars respond to changes in NADPH concentration.
                - paragraph [ref=e312]:
                  - text: To better characterise the pyridine nucleotide specificity of the NAPstar constructs, we titrated NAPstar1, 2, 3, 4, 6 and 7 with varying concentrations of NADPH, NADP
                  - superscript [ref=e313]: +
                  - text: ", NADH or NAD"
                  - superscript [ref=e314]: +
                  - text: (Fig.
                  - link "1c, d, f, g" [ref=e315] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig1
                  - text: ). The NADPH concentration was adjusted from 0.01–1000 µM in the presence of a constant 150 µM NADP
                  - superscript [ref=e316]: +
                  - text: . For all constructs, except for the non-binding control, NAPstarC, and NAPstar4, we observed an NADPH concentration-dependent change in the TS/mC fluorescence emission ratio (Fig.
                  - link "1c" [ref=e317] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig1
                  - text: ). We determined apparent dissociation constants for NADPH (K
                  - subscript [ref=e318]: d(NADPH)
                  - text: ) from the fluorescence changes, which ranged from 0.9 µM for NAPstar1 to 11.6 µM for NAPstar6 (Fig.
                  - link "1e" [ref=e319] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig1
                  - text: ). All NAPstars showed some affinity to NADH, titrated in the presence of 500 µM NAD
                  - superscript [ref=e320]: +
                  - text: ", with K"
                  - subscript [ref=e321]: d(NADH)
                  - text: ranging from 24.4–248.9 µM. However, the affinity for NADH is substantially weaker than that of Peredox (K
                  - subscript [ref=e322]: d(NADH)
                  - text: = 1.2 µM)
                  - superscript [ref=e323]:
                    - link "Reference 23" [ref=e324] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR23
                      - text: "23"
                  - text: and for each NAPstar is one to two orders of magnitude lower than the affinity for NADPH (Fig.
                  - link "1d, e" [ref=e325] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig1
                  - text: ). For all NAPstars, we also observed ratiometric fluorescence responses to NADP
                  - superscript [ref=e326]: +
                  - text: in the opposite direction to those induced by NADPH (Fig.
                  - link "1f" [ref=e327] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig1
                  - text: ). These changes were not observed for NAD
                  - superscript [ref=e328]: +
                  - text: (Fig.
                  - link "1g" [ref=e329] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig1
                  - text: ). These results thus suggest that NAPstars report the bona fide NADP redox state rather than responding solely to the NADPH concentration. To further investigate this, we monitored the dependence of the TS/mC ratio on the NADPH/NADP
                  - superscript [ref=e330]: +
                  - text: ratio at different total NADP (NADPH + NADP
                  - superscript [ref=e331]: +
                  - text: ) pool sizes of 100, 300 and 500 µM respectively (Supplementary Fig.
                  - link "2" [ref=e332] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#MOESM1
                  - text: ). The responses of NAPstar sensors remained largely stable across various pool sizes, indicating a predominant sensitivity to the NADP redox state rather than the individual concentrations of NADPH or NADP
                  - superscript [ref=e333]: +
                  - text: . Exceptions seem to be NAPstar6 and 7, which displayed some dependence on pool size. These two variants also exhibited the highest K
                  - subscript [ref=e334]: d(NADPH)
                  - text: . To reflect the fact that NAPstars measure the NADPH/NADP
                  - superscript [ref=e335]: +
                  - text: ratio, rather than the NADPH concentration, from now on we report K
                  - subscript [ref=e336]: ratios
                  - text: (K
                  - subscript [ref=e337]: r(NADPH/NADP+)
                  - text: ) for every NAPstar instead of K
                  - subscript [ref=e338]: d(NADPH)
                  - text: (Fig.
                  - link "1h" [ref=e339] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig1
                  - text: ).
                - heading "Mixing different Rex domains to enhance the control of NAD(P)H binding affinity" [level=3] [ref=e340]
                - paragraph [ref=e341]:
                  - text: NAPstars contain both Rex domains needed for NADP binding. In contrast, iNaps presumably require dimerisation to form the Rex-dimer for a functional sensor unit, adding sensor concentration (i.e. expression levels) as an additional variable
                  - superscript [ref=e342]:
                    - link "Reference 15" [ref=e343] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR15
                      - text: "15"
                  - text: . We asked if we could exploit the fact that NAPstars encompass a Rex dimer within one polypeptide, by introducing a blend of two different Rex domains into one NAPstar sensor, with the aim to expand the range of accessible NADP redox states. As a first attempt, we created NAPstar3b, which features a single point mutation, V126Y, in the N-terminal NAPstar3-derived Rex domain instead of the usual V130Y mutation which was kept in the second, C-terminal, NAPstar3-derived Rex domain. NAPstar3b was found to have a slightly decreased K
                  - subscript [ref=e344]: d(NADPH)
                  - text: but an approximately 3-fold higher K
                  - subscript [ref=e345]: d(NADH)
                  - text: (Fig.
                  - link "1c–h" [ref=e346] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig1
                  - text: ). Building on this idea, we further pushed the boundaries by combining an N-terminal Rex domain from NAPstar4, a NAPstar variant initially dismissed due to a lack of detectable affinity for NADPH (Fig.
                  - link "1c" [ref=e347] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig1
                  - text: ), with a C-terminal Rex domain from NAPstar3. The resulting construct, NAPstar4.3, was purified as a recombinant protein for in vitro characterisation (Fig.
                  - link "1c–h" [ref=e348] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig1
                  - text: ). Intriguingly, we found that NAPstar4.3 had a strongly decreased affinity for NADPH, K
                  - subscript [ref=e349]: d (NADPH)
                  - text: = 42.6 µM, K
                  - subscript [ref=e350]: r(NADPH/NADP+)
                  - text: = 0.28 (Fig.
                  - link "1c,e" [ref=e351] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig1
                  - text: ), and no detectable affinity for NADH (Fig.
                  - link "1d, e" [ref=e352] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig1
                  - text: ). This suggests that combining different mutated Rex domains within a single NAPstar construct provides enhanced control to rationally modulate NAPstar binding properties.
                - heading "The NAPstars retain the limited pH sensitivity from the Peredox chassis" [level=3] [ref=e353]
                - paragraph [ref=e354]:
                  - text: Resistance of the fluorescence excitation and emission spectra to pH changes is a major achievement of the molecular engineering of the Peredox probe
                  - superscript [ref=e355]:
                    - link "Reference 13" [ref=e356] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR13
                      - text: "13"
                  - text: and there is no reason to believe that this characteristic would differ in the case of NAPstars. To verify whether the NAPstars do indeed retain the low sensitivity to pH as originally introduced into Peredox, we incubated each NAPstar probe in different buffers with pH values ranging from 6.0–9.0 (Supplementary Fig.
                  - link "3a–h" [ref=e357] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#MOESM1
                  - text: ). Each NAPstar was incubated in the absence of NADPH and NADP
                  - superscript [ref=e358]: +
                  - text: ", in the presence of a high NADPH/NADP"
                  - superscript [ref=e359]: +
                  - text: ratio mixture, and in the presence of a low NADPH/NADP
                  - superscript [ref=e360]: +
                  - text: ratio mixture. The TS/mC ratio of each NAPstar varied in a very similar manner as previously observed for Peredox-mCherry
                  - superscript [ref=e361]:
                    - link "Reference 23" [ref=e362] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR23
                      - text: "23"
                  - text: with a less than 20% change in the TS/mC ratio between pH 7.0 and 8.5. The change in TS/mC when NAPstars were incubated with high NADPH/NADP
                  - superscript [ref=e363]: +
                  - text: ratio mixtures was even lower, and remained close to constant between pH 7.0 and 8.5. At more acidic pH, a moderate drop in TS/mC ratio was observed, as previously shown for Peredox
                  - superscript [ref=e364]:
                    - link "Reference 13" [ref=e365] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR13
                      - text: "13"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "Reference 23" [ref=e366] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR23
                      - text: "23"
                  - text: . Nonetheless, the response of NAPstarC mimics the slightly larger response of the NADP
                  - superscript [ref=e367]: +
                  - text: "-bound NAPstar and thus NAPstarC represents a useful control for possible pH effects. As a note of caution, NAPstarC would not allow for control of potential pH-dependent differences in the binding affinity of NAPstars for NADP"
                  - superscript [ref=e368]: +
                  - text: and NADPH. Nonetheless, we expect pH changes to be inconsequential for NAPstars responses in most physiological situations.
                - heading "NAPstars specifically respond to dynamic NADP redox changes in vitro" [level=3] [ref=e369]
                - paragraph [ref=e370]:
                  - text: To further test the dynamic and specific responsiveness of NAPstars to changes in NADP redox status, we used recombinant NAPstar probes to monitor NADP dynamics in enzyme assays in vitro (Supplementary Fig.
                  - link "4" [ref=e371] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#MOESM1
                  - text: ). First, we monitored the reduction of NADP
                  - superscript [ref=e372]: +
                  - text: to NADPH in the isocitrate dehydrogenase-catalysed reaction from isocitrate to α-ketoglutarate (Supplementary Fig.
                  - link "4a" [ref=e373] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#MOESM1
                  - text: ). Upon the addition of isocitrate to start the reaction, the TS/mC ratio of NAPstar1, 2, and 3 rapidly increased. The response rate correlated with K
                  - subscript [ref=e374]: r(NADPH/NADP+)
                  - text: ", i.e. NAPstar1 and 2 responded most rapidly, followed by NAPstar3. No response was detected for NAPstarC. As a further control for the conversion of NADP"
                  - superscript [ref=e375]: +
                  - text: to NADPH, we simultaneously detected an increase in NADPH autofluorescence after initiating the reaction.
                - paragraph [ref=e376]:
                  - text: Next, we monitored NADP oxidation, i.e. the conversion of NADPH to NADP
                  - superscript [ref=e377]: +
                  - text: during the glutathione reductase-catalysed reaction between NADPH and glutathione disulfide (GSSG) (Supplementary Fig.
                  - link "4b" [ref=e378] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#MOESM1
                  - text: ). Upon the addition of GSSG, we observed a rapid decrease in the TS/mC ratio for each NAPstar construct except NAPstarC. Among the tested NAPstars, NAPstar3, which has the highest K
                  - subscript [ref=e379]: r(NADPH/NADP+)
                  - text: ", responded first to NADPH consumption, followed by NAPstar1 and NAPstar2."
                - paragraph [ref=e380]:
                  - text: Finally, we used NAPstars and Peredox to monitor the conversion of NADPH to NADH, which cannot be resolved using standard NAD(P)H autofluorescence (Supplementary Fig.
                  - link "4c" [ref=e381] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#MOESM1
                  - text: ). Upon initiating the reaction with alkaline phosphatase, we observed a decrease in the TS/mC ratio, first for NAPstar3, followed by NAPstar2, and finally NAPstar1. The TS/mC of Peredox increased rapidly following the initiation of the reaction, consistent with an increase in the NADH concentration. In summary, these results demonstrate that NAPstars and Peredox allow the selective monitoring of NADP and NAD redox dynamics, respectively.
                - heading "The NAPstars are well-suited to FLIM measurements" [level=3] [ref=e382]
                - paragraph [ref=e383]:
                  - text: Fluorescence lifetime imaging (FLIM) is a powerful alternative approach to monitor the status of fluorescent molecules and proteins and can be especially advantageous, for example in situations of low signal-to-noise at the fluorescence intensity level or where there are overlapping fluorescence excitation and emission spectra. We thus sought to test the suitability of NAPstar sensors for FLIM measurements (Supplementary Fig.
                  - link "5" [ref=e384] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#MOESM1
                  - text: ). To this end, we monitored the fluorescence lifetime of NAPstar4.3 in the presence of different NADPH/NADP
                  - superscript [ref=e385]: +
                  - text: ratios. We observed a change in the fluorescence lifetime from 1.3 ns in an NADP
                  - superscript [ref=e386]: +
                  - text: "-saturated state to 2.3 ns in an NADPH-saturated state, which is exceptionally large for a fluorescent protein-based biosensor. Based on the FLIM data, we determined a K"
                  - subscript [ref=e387]: d(NADPH)
                  - text: = 40.5 µM and K
                  - subscript [ref=e388]: r(NADPH/NADP+)
                  - text: = 0.27, which are very close to the 42.6 µM and 0.28 values determined by fluorescence intensity measurements (Fig.
                  - link "1" [ref=e389] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig1
                  - text: ). We conclude that NAPstars have favourable characteristics for the development of FLIM-based measurement approaches.
                - heading "Cytosolic NADP redox homoeostasis is robustly maintained in yeast" [level=3] [ref=e390]
                - paragraph [ref=e391]:
                  - text: Given that NAPstars are indeed specific sensors of the NADP redox state in vitro, we next sought to test them in various eukaryotic model systems, starting with the budding yeast, Saccharomyces cerevisiae. We used a microplate-based assay to monitor the response of all NAPstars and PeredoxDS (an affinity variant of Peredox-mCherry for more reducing NAD redox states)
                  - superscript [ref=e392]:
                    - link "Reference 23" [ref=e393] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR23
                      - text: "23"
                  - text: ", expressed in the yeast cytosol, to exogenous H"
                  - subscript [ref=e394]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e395]: "2"
                  - text: at initial concentrations ranging from 0–5 mM, as well as tert-butylperoxide (t-BuOOH) at 0–1 mM to avoid NADP-independent scavenging by catalase (Fig.
                  - link "2" [ref=e396] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig2
                  - text: and Supplementary Figs.
                  - link "6" [ref=e397] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#MOESM1
                  - text: and
                  - link "7" [ref=e398] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#MOESM1
                  - text: ). Strikingly, even at the highest H
                  - subscript [ref=e399]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e400]: "2"
                  - text: concentration, a minimal response was detected for NAPstar1, 2 or 3 (Fig.
                  - link "2c" [ref=e401] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig2
                  - text: and Supplementary Fig.
                  - link "6" [ref=e402] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#MOESM1
                  - text: ) Similar results were observed with t-BuOOH (Supplementary Fig.
                  - link "7" [ref=e403] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#MOESM1
                  - text: ). NAPstar6 and 7, with a high K
                  - subscript [ref=e404]: r(NADPH/NADP+)
                  - text: ", showed a limited response with a rapid recovery, while the TS/mC ratio of NAPstar4.3 was close to that of NAPstarC (Supplementary Fig."
                  - link "6" [ref=e405] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#MOESM1
                  - text: ). These observations are consistent with the relative affinities of the sensor variants determined in vitro (Fig.
                  - link "1" [ref=e406] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig1
                  - text: ) and suggest robust and active maintenance of the cytosolic NADP redox state even under severe oxidative challenge, which is in stark contrast to previous observations of a highly volatile cytosolic NAD redox state as monitored by Peredox
                  - superscript [ref=e407]:
                    - link "Reference 13" [ref=e408] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR13
                      - text: "13"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "Reference 23" [ref=e409] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR23
                      - text: "23"
                  - text: . The similarity in the fluorescence ratio of NADP
                  - superscript [ref=e410]: +
                  - text: "-bound and unbound NAPstars (as for NAPstarC) is consistent with previously published crystal structures of Thermotoga maritima Rex (TmRex) in NADH-bound, NAD"
                  - superscript [ref=e411]: +
                  - text: "-bound, and unbound (apo) states"
                  - superscript [ref=e412]:
                    - link "Reference 24" [ref=e413] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR24
                      - text: "24"
                  - text: . Unbound and NAD
                  - superscript [ref=e414]: +
                  - text: "-bound TmRex are structurally very similar, while NADH binding induces significant structural changes. This suggests that NAPstar4.3 is in an almost fully NADP"
                  - superscript [ref=e415]: +
                  - text: "-bound state in the yeast cytosol and is unable to respond to oxidation of the NADP redox couple (Supplementary Fig."
                  - link "6" [ref=e416] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#MOESM1
                  - text: ). The measurements allowed for an in vivo estimate for the NADPH/NADP
                  - superscript [ref=e417]: +
                  - text: ratio in the cultured yeast cytosol in the range of 0.03–0.3 (Figs.
                  - link "1" [ref=e418] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig1
                  - text: ","
                  - link "2" [ref=e419] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig2
                  - text: and Supplementary Fig.
                  - link "6" [ref=e420] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#MOESM1
                  - text: ).
                - 'figure "Fig. 2: Cytosolic NADP redox homoeostasis is robustly maintained." [ref=e422]':
                  - generic [ref=e424]: "Fig. 2: Cytosolic NADP redox homoeostasis is robustly maintained."
                  - generic [ref=e425]:
                    - generic [ref=e426]:
                      - 'img "Fig. 2: Cytosolic NADP redox homoeostasis is robustly maintained." [ref=e428]'
                      - link "Full size image figure 2" [ref=e430] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x/figures/2
                        - text: Full size image
                        - img [ref=e431]
                    - paragraph [ref=e434]:
                      - text: a Epifluorescence microscopy images of yeast cells expressing NAPstar3, showing cpT-Sapphire and mCherry fluorescence as well as brightfield microscopy. Representative image; images were obtained from three independent yeast cultures. b–g Response of PeredoxDS, NAPstar3 and NAPstarC probes, expressed in the cytosol of wild-type and Δzwf1 yeast cells, to the addition of exogenous H
                      - subscript [ref=e435]: "2"
                      - text: O
                      - subscript [ref=e436]: "2"
                      - text: at the indicated concentrations (n = 3 experimental repeats in which H
                      - subscript [ref=e437]: "2"
                      - text: O
                      - subscript [ref=e438]: "2"
                      - text: responses were monitored in cells derived from independent cultures. Identical experimental settings were used for all panels allowing for direct comparison of TS/mC between datasets. h Epifluorescence microscopy images showing cpT-Sapphire and mCherry fluorescence of NAPstar3b expressed in the cytosol of HeLa cells. Representative image; images were obtained from four separate HeLa cell cultures. i Response to NAPstar3b (n = 148 individual cells for the starved condition and n = 151 individual cells for the glucose condition, monitored in both cases in the course of four experimental replicates) and HyPer7 (n = 144 individual cells for the starved condition and n = 154 individual cells for the glucose condition, monitored in both cases in the course of n = 3 experimental replicates) probes, expressed in the cytosol of HeLa cells cultured in a perfusion chamber, to the perfusion of buffers with stepwise increases in H
                      - subscript [ref=e439]: "2"
                      - text: O
                      - subscript [ref=e440]: "2"
                      - text: concentration. Within each experimental replicate, the response was determined as the mean of the individual cell responses. Data are presented as the mean of experimental replicates with error bars representing the standard deviation between experimental replicates.
                - paragraph [ref=e441]:
                  - text: To distinguish between a bona fide robustness of cytosolic NADP redox maintenance and a potential lack of probe functionality in yeast, we sought to make NAPstar measurements in yeast cells with constrained cytosolic NADPH regeneration. To this end, we monitored the NAPstar responses in a Δzwf1 yeast strain. ZWF1 encodes glucose 6-phosphate dehydrogenase, the first enzyme in the pentose phosphate pathway, which is the major source of cytosolic NADPH in yeast. In Δzwf1 cells, we observed sensitive, H
                  - subscript [ref=e442]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e443]: "2"
                  - text: and t-BuOOH concentration-dependent responses for all NAPstar constructs, supporting the conclusion that cytosolic NADP redox homoeostasis is robustly maintained in wild-type yeast by adjusting flux through the oxidative pentose phosphate pathway (Fig.
                  - link "2" [ref=e444] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig2
                  - text: and Supplementary Fig.
                  - link "6" [ref=e445] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#MOESM1
                  - text: )
                  - superscript [ref=e446]:
                    - link "25" [ref=e447] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#ref-CR25"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "26" [ref=e448] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#ref-CR26"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "27" [ref=e449] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#ref-CR27"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "Reference 28" [ref=e450] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR28
                      - text: "28"
                  - text: . This finding is reminiscent of our previous observations of the robustness of cytosolic glutathione redox homoeostasis, which largely relies on continuous NADPH supply, indicating that the robustness in NADP redox state also serves the stability of downstream redox pools
                  - superscript [ref=e451]:
                    - link "Reference 21" [ref=e452] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR21
                      - text: "21"
                  - text: . NAPstarC did not respond in either WT or Δzwf1 cells (Fig.
                  - link "2d, e" [ref=e453] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig2
                  - text: ) whilst Peredox responded similarly in both strains (Fig.
                  - link "2f, g" [ref=e454] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig2
                  - text: ).
                - heading "The robustness of cytosolic NADP redox homoeostasis is conserved" [level=3] [ref=e455]
                - paragraph [ref=e456]:
                  - text: We next sought to understand to what extent the principles of NADP redox maintenance apply beyond yeast. Hence, we introduced and validated NAPstars in additional eukaryotic models. To this end, we used transient transfection to introduce NAPstar3b into HeLa cells (Fig.
                  - link "2h" [ref=e457] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig2
                  - text: ). NAPstar3b was chosen as due to its K
                  - subscript [ref=e458]: r(NADPH/NADP+)
                  - text: we expected it to be almost fully NADPH bound at steady state, therefore allowing for the maximum possible deflection upon NADP oxidation, and due to its particularly low NADH binding affinity. We were first interested in monitoring the response of NAPstar3b to exogenous H
                  - subscript [ref=e459]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e460]: "2"
                  - text: in cells subjected to glucose starvation, comparing them to cells continuously exposed to 10 mM glucose. In a perfusion chamber, we monitored the response of HeLa cells to stepwise increases in H
                  - subscript [ref=e461]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e462]: "2"
                  - text: concentration (Fig.
                  - link "2i" [ref=e463] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig2
                  - text: ). In glucose-starved cells, we observed a stepwise increase in NADP oxidation (Fig.
                  - link "2i" [ref=e464] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig2
                  - text: ). In contrast, in non-glucose starved cells, with the presence of 10 mM glucose in all perfusion buffers, no NADP oxidation was observed, even with continuous perfusion of buffer containing 20 µM H
                  - subscript [ref=e465]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e466]: "2"
                  - text: (Fig.
                  - link "2i" [ref=e467] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig2
                  - text: ). Additionally, we used the ultra-sensitive H
                  - subscript [ref=e468]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e469]: "2"
                  - text: probe HyPer7
                  - superscript [ref=e470]:
                    - link "Reference 29" [ref=e471] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR29
                      - text: "29"
                  - text: to monitor H
                  - subscript [ref=e472]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e473]: "2"
                  - text: in the same experimental setup (Fig.
                  - link "2i" [ref=e474] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig2
                  - text: ). Consistent with the NAPstar response, we observed almost no HyPer7 response in glucose-fed cells, except at the highest H
                  - subscript [ref=e475]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e476]: "2"
                  - text: concentration, suggesting that HeLa cells can very efficiently remove H
                  - subscript [ref=e477]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e478]: "2"
                  - text: as long as reductant can be efficiently delivered by metabolism. In contrast, in glucose-starved cells, we observed a strong HyPer7 response beginning with the lowest H
                  - subscript [ref=e479]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e480]: "2"
                  - text: concentration tested.
                - paragraph [ref=e481]:
                  - text: In summary, we found that, akin to the situation in yeast, cytosolic NADP redox homoeostasis in HeLa cells is robustly maintained, and NADPH is readily available for the reduction of exogenous H
                  - subscript [ref=e482]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e483]: "2"
                  - text: . Robustly regulated cytosolic NADP redox homoeostasis hence may be a conserved physiological characteristic from yeast to human cells.
                - heading "Yeast cell cycle is accompanied by oscillations in NAD and NADP redox states" [level=3] [ref=e484]
                - paragraph [ref=e485]:
                  - text: Confident that NAPstars and Peredox faithfully report changes in NADP and NAD redox states within the yeast cytosol, we tested whether we could use NAPstars and Peredox to investigate the existence of dynamic changes in pyridine nucleotide redox states during the yeast metabolic cycle (YMC). The YMC is a phenomenon observed in continuous yeast cultures under mild glucose limitation, involving synchronised metabolic and transcriptional cycles along with cell cycle synchronisation
                  - superscript [ref=e486]:
                    - link "Reference 30" [ref=e487] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR30
                      - text: "30"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "Reference 31" [ref=e488] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR31
                      - text: "31"
                  - text: . Previous reports have suggested that the total NAD(H) and NADP(H) levels change during the YMC
                  - superscript [ref=e489]:
                    - link "Reference 32" [ref=e490] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR32
                      - text: "32"
                  - text: ", or have shown changes in NAD(P)H"
                  - superscript [ref=e491]:
                    - link "Reference 33" [ref=e492] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR33
                      - text: "33"
                  - text: . However, measurement of changes in NADP, or NAD redox states, and the examination of specific subcellular NAD(P) pools has hitherto been impossible in this system.
                - paragraph [ref=e493]:
                  - text: To address this issue, we established continuous yeast cultures
                  - superscript [ref=e494]:
                    - link "Reference 34" [ref=e495] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR34
                      - text: "34"
                  - text: with yeast cells expressing Peredox, NAPstar4.3, or HyPer7
                  - superscript [ref=e496]:
                    - link "Reference 29" [ref=e497] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR29
                      - text: "29"
                  - text: . We selected NAPstar4.3 after testing different NAPstar variants for the specific conditions of the fermentor cultures, as this sensor variant showed optimal performance, particularly in terms of allowing fully dynamic measurements, unlike in more nutrient-rich batch cultures where it is almost fully NADP
                  - superscript [ref=e498]: +
                  - text: bound (Fig.
                  - link "3" [ref=e499] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig3
                  - text: and Supplementary Fig.
                  - link "8" [ref=e500] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#MOESM1
                  - text: ). Using a coupled fermentor–fluorimeter setup to continuously monitor probe fluorescence within the cells in the fermentor culture (Fig.
                  - link "3a, b" [ref=e501] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig3
                  - text: )
                  - superscript [ref=e502]:
                    - link "Reference 34" [ref=e503] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR34
                      - text: "34"
                  - text: ", we found periodic oscillations in both NAD and NADP redox states (Fig."
                  - link "3c" [ref=e504] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig3
                  - text: ). These oscillations were synchronised with fluctuations in oxygen consumption, which are well understood to be coupled to cell division
                  - superscript [ref=e505]:
                    - link "Reference 31" [ref=e506] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR31
                      - text: "31"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "Reference 32" [ref=e507] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR32
                      - text: "32"
                  - text: . HyPer7 responses confirmed the cell cycle and metabolic cycle-associated H
                  - subscript [ref=e508]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e509]: "2"
                  - text: cycles that we previously reported, based on roGFP2-Tsa2ΔC
                  - subscript [ref=e510]: R
                  - text: sensor responses
                  - superscript [ref=e511]:
                    - link "Reference 34" [ref=e512] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR34
                      - text: "34"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "Reference 35" [ref=e513] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR35
                      - text: "35"
                  - text: . Interestingly, our measurements showed that the three different redox species respond independently of each other, are not in equilibrium, and need to be analysed separately. For example, the peak reduction of the NADP pool coincided with the highest H
                  - subscript [ref=e514]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e515]: "2"
                  - text: levels. Likewise, the reduction and oxidation phases of the NAD and NADP pools occur at different time-points. These experiments highlight the complex and highly dynamic redox landscape of the cell, where individual redox couples, even within a single subcellular compartment, often do not equilibrate with each other in vivo. Those data re-emphasise the fact that there is no such thing as a general cellular redox state and the critical need for the specific empirical measurement of individual redox couples.
                - 'figure "Fig. 3: Oscillations in cytosolic NADP and NAD redox state accompany the yeast metabolic cycle (YMC)." [ref=e517]':
                  - generic [ref=e519]: "Fig. 3: Oscillations in cytosolic NADP and NAD redox state accompany the yeast metabolic cycle (YMC)."
                  - generic [ref=e520]:
                    - generic [ref=e521]:
                      - 'img "Fig. 3: Oscillations in cytosolic NADP and NAD redox state accompany the yeast metabolic cycle (YMC)." [ref=e523]'
                      - link "Full size image figure 3" [ref=e525] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x/figures/3
                        - text: Full size image
                        - img [ref=e526]
                    - paragraph [ref=e529]:
                      - text: a Photograph of the coupled fermentor–fluorimeter setup used to monitor redox changes in YMC-synchronised cultures. b Diagram illustrating the coupled metabolic and cell division cycles observed during the YMC. CDC (cell division cycle), HOC (high oxygen consumption), and LOC (low oxygen consumption). c Representative traces showing the changes in dissolved oxygen, NAPstar4.3 (NADP redox state), Peredox (NAD redox state), and Hyper7 (H
                      - subscript [ref=e530]: "2"
                      - text: O
                      - subscript [ref=e531]: "2"
                      - text: ) during two complete cycles of the YMC (n = 2, in which probe dynamics were measured for multiple YMC cycles in two independent YMC-synchronised cultures; Supplementary Fig.
                      - link "7" [ref=e532] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#MOESM1
                      - text: ).
                - heading "NAPstars reveal NADP redox responses in the plant cytosol to illumination and hypoxia" [level=3] [ref=e533]
                - paragraph [ref=e534]:
                  - text: Next, we aimed to enable NAPstar-based measurements of the NADP redox state in plants, which plays a crucial role in underpinning photosynthesis and stress responses. Despite its importance in crop improvement, the specific dynamics of compartmentalised NADP redox state have been difficult to assess
                  - superscript [ref=e535]:
                    - link "36" [ref=e536] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#ref-CR36"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "37" [ref=e537] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#ref-CR37"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "Reference 38" [ref=e538] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR38
                      - text: "38"
                  - text: . The few subcellular fractionation datasets that are available have been suggestive of only minor NADP redox changes at dark-light changes in the cytosolic fractions, which appears in stark contrast to pronounced NADP redox dynamics in the chloroplast, and indeed NAD redox dynamics in the cytosol
                  - superscript [ref=e539]:
                    - link "36" [ref=e540] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#ref-CR36"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "37" [ref=e541] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#ref-CR37"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "Reference 38" [ref=e542] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR38
                      - text: "38"
                  - text: . Using Arabidopsis thaliana as a model, we generated stable transgenic NAPstar3, NAPstar4.3 and NAPstarC lines with cyto-nuclear sensor expression (Fig.
                  - link "4a" [ref=e543] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig4
                  - text: and Supplementary Fig.
                  - link "9" [ref=e544] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#MOESM1
                  - text: ). NAPstar3 and 4.3 were chosen as their different K
                  - subscript [ref=e545]: r(NADPH/NADP+)
                  - text: was expected to allow us to cover a broad-range of plausible cytosolic NADP redox states. The steady-state TS/mC ratio of NAPstar3 was higher than for NAPstar4.3 reflecting the lower K
                  - subscript [ref=e546]: r(NADPH/NADP+)
                  - text: of NAPstar3. Consistent with our observations in yeast, the difference in NADPH occupancy suggested a close match of the response ranges of both sensor variants with the physiological NADPH:NADP
                  - superscript [ref=e547]: +
                  - text: ratio (Supplementary Fig.
                  - link "9" [ref=e548] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#MOESM1
                  - text: ), which may be estimated at about 0.3.
                - 'figure "Fig. 4: Cytosolic redox dynamics accompany illumination and hypoxia-reoxygenation in plants." [ref=e550]':
                  - generic [ref=e552]: "Fig. 4: Cytosolic redox dynamics accompany illumination and hypoxia-reoxygenation in plants."
                  - generic [ref=e553]:
                    - generic [ref=e554]:
                      - 'img "Fig. 4: Cytosolic redox dynamics accompany illumination and hypoxia-reoxygenation in plants." [ref=e556]'
                      - link "Full size image figure 4" [ref=e558] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x/figures/4
                        - text: Full size image
                        - img [ref=e559]
                    - paragraph [ref=e562]: a Confocal microscopy images of NAPstar4.3 expressed in the cytosol of Arabidopsis thaliana plants. Scale bar = 20 µm. Response of NAPstar4.3 (b), NAPstarC (c) and Peredox (d) to the indicated periods of illumination after treatment with a solvent control (Mock) or the photosynthetic inhibitor DCMU (in each panel data presented are the mean ± s.d. based on n = 6 leaf discs from six individual plants). e, Box and whisker plot, derived from the datasets in b–d, showing the change in the normalised log10 TS/mC ratio after 60 minutes of illumination. Boxes show the interquartile range, with the middle line defining the median. X, represents the mean values. Whiskers show the minimum and maximum values, excluding outliers. Dots indicate outlier values, which are defined as being 1.5 times the interquartile range above and below the third and first quartile respectively. P-values are derived from an unpaired two-tailed Student’s t-test. Response of NAPstar4.3 (f), NAPstarC (g), Peredox (h), Grx1-roGFP2 (i) and roGFP2-Orp1 (j) probes to 6 hours of hypoxia (0.1% oxygen) followed by restoration of normal atmospheric oxygen levels (n = 7 for NAPstarC, roGFP2-Orp1 and Grx1-roGFP2, n = 8 for Peredox and NAPstar4.3leaf discs taken from 7 or 8 individual plants). In all panels, data are presented as mean ± s.d. normalised to the average value before induction of hypoxia.
                - paragraph [ref=e563]:
                  - text: To assess the in vivo responsiveness of the sensors, we illuminated leaf tissue, reasoning that activation of photosynthesis triggers the exchange of redox equivalents between the chloroplast stroma and the cytosol by different metabolite shuttles (Fig.
                  - link "4" [ref=e564] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig4
                  - text: )
                  - superscript [ref=e565]:
                    - link "Reference 39" [ref=e566] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR39
                      - text: "39"
                  - text: . Consecutive illumination of discs from mature Arabidopsis leaves, for periods of 5, 15, 30 and 60 min, each of which was followed by fluorimetric time-lapse measurements, revealed a pronounced reduction of the cytosolic NAD pool as monitored by Peredox (Fig.
                  - link "4d" [ref=e567] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig4
                  - text: )
                  - superscript [ref=e568]:
                    - link "Reference 23" [ref=e569] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR23
                      - text: "23"
                  - text: . The amplitude of NAD reduction increased slightly with the duration of illumination (Fig.
                  - link "4d" [ref=e570] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig4
                  - text: ). In contrast, NAPstar4.3 did not exhibit cytosolic NADP reduction after 5 and 15 min of illumination (Fig.
                  - link "4b" [ref=e571] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig4
                  - text: ). A slight, but reproducible sensor response beyond that of the NAPStarC control was observable only after longer illumination periods, i.e. 30 and 60 min, indicating a degree of cytosolic NADP reduction (Fig.
                  - link "4b" [ref=e572] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig4
                  - text: ). The response was decreased, albeit not completely abolished by the photosynthetic inhibitor 3-(3,4-dichlorophenyl)-1,1-dimethylurea (DCMU) (Fig.
                  - link "4b–e" [ref=e573] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig4
                  - text: ), indicating a contribution of photosynthetic electron transport to the response. Overall, the dynamics observed for the NAPstars were minor as compared to the response of Peredox, confirming the respective specificity of the two sensors for NADP and NAD redox status in the plant cytosol and validating previous indications from subcellular fractionation measurements that only minor NADP redox changes occur in the cytosol at the onset of illumination
                  - superscript [ref=e574]:
                    - link "36" [ref=e575] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#ref-CR36"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "37" [ref=e576] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#ref-CR37"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "Reference 38" [ref=e577] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR38
                      - text: "38"
                  - text: . The NAPstarC control also showed changes, indicating an NADP-independent effect in illuminated leaf tissue, which we did not observe in the other models. These changes may however be corrected for by subtraction (not performed here in the interest of full data transparency) (Fig.
                  - link "4b,c" [ref=e578] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig4
                  - text: ). Based on the observation that NAPstar4.3 allows dynamic measurements of both reductive and oxidative changes in the cytosolic NADP pool, we selected NAPstar4.3 as suitable sensor variant for further measurements in plant leaf tissue.
                - paragraph [ref=e579]:
                  - text: We next explored NADP redox dynamics in response to hypoxia stress, which causes a cellular redox crisis. Hypoxia-dependent inhibition of respiration leads to NAD reduction, as previously observed in living leaf tissue
                  - superscript [ref=e580]:
                    - link "Reference 40" [ref=e581] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR40
                      - text: "40"
                  - text: . The impact of hypoxia on cytosolic NADP redox dynamics has remained less clear since lack of oxygen as an electron sink causes metabolic reduction but also boosts H
                  - subscript [ref=e582]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e583]: "2"
                  - text: production and glutathione oxidation in different phases of the hypoxic episode
                  - superscript [ref=e584]:
                    - link "Reference 41" [ref=e585] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR41
                      - text: "41"
                  - text: . Lowering oxygen levels to 5%, 1% and 0.1% for 6 h before reoxygenation to ambient levels confirmed rapid and reversible NAD reduction in Arabidopsis leaf tissue at 1 and 0.1% oxygen (Fig.
                  - link "4f–j" [ref=e586] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig4
                  - text: and Supplementary Fig.
                  - link "10" [ref=e587] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#MOESM1
                  - text: ). Interestingly, the NADP redox state, as monitored by NAPstar 4.3, responded oppositely to the NAD redox state. After a lag phase, the NADP pool was gradually oxidised. At the technical level this response validates the strict specificity of NAPstar4.3 for the NADP, and not NAD, redox state in planta. At the physiological level, it shows that the redox dynamics of the NADP and NAD pools are strictly independent in the plant cytosol, which is in line with the absence of any transhydrogenase gene homologue in the plant genome.
                - paragraph [ref=e588]:
                  - text: We next hypothesised that NADP oxidation during hypoxia may be due to NADPH consumption by the antioxidant machinery which is not matched by the rate of resupply due to limitations in flux through central metabolism. To test this hypothesis, we also measured glutathione redox potential and H
                  - subscript [ref=e589]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e590]: "2"
                  - text: dynamics using Grx1-roGFP2 and roGFP2-Orp1, respectively. Both sensors showed increased oxidation during 1% and 0.1% hypoxia, which correlated with NADP oxidation, suggesting efficient equilibration between NADP and glutathione redox status via glutathione reductase in the plant cytosol. This correlation was lost upon reoxygenation, however, as the NADP pool was rapidly reduced, while both Grx1-roGFP2 and roGFP2-Orp1 reported a burst in oxidation (Fig.
                  - link "4f–j" [ref=e591] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig4
                  - text: ). This observation might be explained by a burst of H
                  - subscript [ref=e592]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e593]: "2"
                  - text: production during reoxygenation, while metabolic NADP reduction is efficiently restored. The reduction of the NADP pool suggests that the electron influx into the NADP pool by metabolism more than compensates for the electron efflux to the antioxidant systems, and that glutathione reductase activity, catalysing the reduction of glutathione from NADPH, is limiting under the specific conditions of reoxygenation after hypoxia.
                - paragraph [ref=e594]:
                  - text: We reasoned that the diametrically opposed redox responses of the NADP and glutathione pools to reoxygenation, i.e. NADP reduction and glutathione oxidation, represented an opportunity to benchmark the recently introduced biosensor NERNST against NAPstars and roGFP2-Grx1 probes in vivo. NERNST is based on a redox-sensitive GFP (roGFP2), which is known to respond sensitively to changes in the glutathione redox state (E
                  - subscript [ref=e595]: GSH
                  - text: ) in a reaction efficiently catalysed by endogenous glutaredoxins in most cell compartments, including the plant cytosol
                  - superscript [ref=e596]:
                    - link "19" [ref=e597] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#ref-CR19"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "20" [ref=e598] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#ref-CR20"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "21" [ref=e599] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#ref-CR21"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "Reference 22" [ref=e600] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR22
                      - text: "22"
                  - text: . This raises the crucial question of whether NERNST truly reports the NADP redox state in vivo or whether its specificity is compromised by catalysed equilibration with E
                  - subscript [ref=e601]: GSH
                  - text: . We thus repeated the hypoxia-reoxygenation experiments in leaves with NAPstar4.3, NAPstarC, Peredox, roGFP2-Grx1 and NERNST sensors (Supplementary Fig.
                  - link "11" [ref=e602] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#MOESM1
                  - text: ) The NERNST response was almost indistinguishable from the roGFP2-Grx1 response (Supplementary Fig.
                  - link "11c,d" [ref=e603] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#MOESM1
                  - text: ) consistent with NERNST responding to changes in E
                  - subscript [ref=e604]: GSH
                  - text: in the Arabidopsis cytosol. Since high glutaredoxin activity, through several different glutaredoxin isoforms, is common in the cytosol of plants in general, our data indicates that NERNST is not an NADP redox state sensor in plants and functions primarily as an E
                  - subscript [ref=e605]: GSH
                  - text: reporter.
                - heading "The glutathione pathway mediates high capacity NADPH-dependent antioxidative electron flux" [level=3] [ref=e606]
                - paragraph [ref=e607]:
                  - text: Having established the functionality of NAPstars in yeast, mammalian cells and plants, we next sought to address a longstanding question in redox biology, namely, the role of glutathione in cellular antioxidative responses. Although glutathione is often loosely referred to as a key cellular antioxidant, its mechanistic roles within cells have been remarkably difficult to pin down and a matter of longstanding debate. Importantly, the thioredoxin pathway is considered to be the primary reductive system, with glutathione seen as having a backup or auxiliary role, particularly in yeast and mammalian cells
                  - superscript [ref=e608]:
                    - link "42" [ref=e609] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#ref-CR42"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "43" [ref=e610] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#ref-CR43"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "Reference 44" [ref=e611] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR44
                      - text: "44"
                  - text: .
                - paragraph [ref=e612]:
                  - text: Upon exposure to exogenous oxidants, such as H
                  - subscript [ref=e613]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e614]: "2"
                  - text: or diamide, there is a strongly increased demand for NADPH. Both the thioredoxin reductase/thioredoxin and glutathione reductase/glutathione/glutaredoxin pathways (ascorbate-glutathione cycle in plants)
                  - superscript [ref=e615]:
                    - link "Reference 45" [ref=e616] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#ref-CR45
                      - text: "45"
                  - text: use electrons from NADPH to reduce cellular disulfide bonds and certain reactive oxygen and reactive nitrogen species. Nonetheless, monitoring the relative flux of electrons through these two pathways in vivo under pro-oxidative conditions has proven challenging. To address the question of the relative contribution to antioxidant electron flux we made use of genetic or chemical inhibition of the glutathione and thioredoxin-dependent pathways and monitored the cytosolic NADP redox dynamics in response to exogenous oxidants in living cells and tissues.
                - paragraph [ref=e617]:
                  - text: First, we examined yeast cells. We monitored the response of NAPstar3, NAPstarC, and PeredoxDS probes expressed in wild-type cells, in cells deleted for the GLR1 gene (encoding glutathione reductase), and in cells deleted for TRX1 and TRX2 (encoding the two cytosolic thioredoxins). The probe responses in all cells were monitored in response to the addition of exogenous diamide at concentrations ranging from 0–5 mM (Fig.
                  - link "5a–e" [ref=e618] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig5
                  - text: ). Diamide was chosen instead of H
                  - subscript [ref=e619]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e620]: "2"
                  - text: due to the extremely small deflections in cytosolic NADPH redox homoostasis detected in yeast in response to peroxides (Fig.
                  - link "2" [ref=e621] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig2
                  - text: and Supplementary Figs.
                  - link "6" [ref=e622] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#MOESM1
                  - text: and
                  - link "7" [ref=e623] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#MOESM1
                  - text: ). In wild-type cells, we observed a strong oxidation of the cytosolic NADP pool upon treatment with 2 and 5 mM diamide (Fig.
                  - link "5b" [ref=e624] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig5
                  - text: ). Intriguingly however, in Δglr1 cells, we observed almost no detectable NADP oxidation at all, except for a slight response of the NAPstar3 probe to 5 mM diamide (Fig.
                  - link "5c,e" [ref=e625] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig5
                  - text: ). Deletion of both cytosolic thioredoxins also led to less severe NADP oxidation following diamide treatment in comparison to that observed in wild-type cells (Fig.
                  - link "5d, e" [ref=e626] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig5
                  - text: ). However, the change of the NAPstar3 TS/mC ratio was much greater than that observed in Δglr1 cells (Fig.
                  - link "5e" [ref=e627] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#Fig5
                  - text: ). The response of PeredoxDS was similar in wild-type, Δglr1 and Δtrx1Δtrx2 cells, while NAPstarC showed no response in all cases (Supplementary Fig.
                  - link "12" [ref=e628] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: /articles/s41467-024-55302-x#MOESM1
                  - text: ). Our results indicate that genetic impairment of both the thioredoxin and glutathione-dependent pathways can hinder the consumption of NADPH upon pro-oxidative challenges in the yeast cytosol, but the impact of blocking the glutathione pathway is more pronounced.
                - 'figure "Fig. 5: Glutathione reductase deletion protects against diamide-induced NADP oxidation in yeast." [ref=e630]':
                  - generic [ref=e632]: "Fig. 5: Glutathione reductase deletion protects against diamide-induced NADP oxidation in yeast."
